<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full picture. Let me analyze:

**v1 baselines** (batch_size=4, 30K steps, single GPU):
- baseline_s1: 30K steps, loss=0.0244, TSR=0% on SafeLIBERO spatial (from eval logs)
- baseline_s2: 30K steps, loss=0.0264, TSR=0% on SafeLIBERO spatial (from eval logs)
- baseline_s3: 30K steps, loss=0.048

**v2 baselines** (batch_size=128, 5K steps, DDP 2-GPU):


- baseline_v2_s1: 5K steps, loss=0.0147, TSR=0% on sanity check

This is striking—the v1 baselines achieved TSR=0% even after 30K steps with batch_size=4, which contradicts the assumption that smaller batch sizes were the culprit. The v1 training accumulated 120K total samples (4 × 30K), yet still showed zero task success rate, suggesting the issue runs deeper than just batch size configuration.

Interestingly, v2 actually processed more data—640K samples across 5K steps with batch_size=128—but still hit TSR=0%. This makes me wonder whether the fundamental training setup is flawed or if π₀.₅ simply requires substantially more fine-tuning to adapt to LIBERO's specific task structure. The low loss values indicate the model is fitting the flow-matching objective, but that metric doesn't necessarily translate to task performance, so I should check whether the team has consulted the original π₀.₅ paper or openpi's reference configurations for LIBERO fine-tuning.

The consistent TSR=0% across all baselines—v1 at 30K steps, v2 at 5K, multiple seeds—is a red flag that suggests this isn't simply a matter of training longer. The "Too many contacts" warnings during evaluation point to erratic robot movements rather than graceful task failures, which hints at deeper misalignments: action normalization discrepancies between training and inference, action chunk horizon mismatches, observation preprocessing differences, missing camera views, or ODE solver configuration issues. Since v1 already failed at 30K steps, the ROADMAP's Plan B to resume to 10K is unlikely to solve the problem—the issue probably lies elsewhere in the pipeline, not training duration.

The batch size hypothesis is now falsified: v1 with batch_size=4 had TSR=0, and v2 with batch_size=128 also has TSR=0, despite v2 processing 640K samples versus v1's 120K in roughly the same wall-clock time. This points to a critical problem in the evaluation or inference setup rather than the training process itself. I need to verify that the eval configuration exactly matches training—action normalization, chunk horizon, camera views—and consider testing inference on a known-good checkpoint to isolate whether the issue is in the model or the evaluation pipeline.
```

### Assistant

_source line 3341 · 2026-05-20T12:42:19.143Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Clear pattern: ALL baseline evaluations across ALL seeds and ALL SafeLIBERO suites show TSR=0.00%. This includes:

v1 baselines (30K steps, bs=4):
- baseline_s1: SafeLIBERO-Spatial TSR=0%, SafeLIBERO-Object TSR=0%, SafeLIBERO-Goal TSR=0%, SafeLIBERO-Long TSR=0%


- baseline_s2: SafeLIBERO-Spatial TSR=0%, SafeLIBERO-Object TSR=0%

v2 baseline (5K steps, bs=128):
- baseline_v2_s1: Sanity check TSR=0%

The CAR metric varies widely (20-90%), so the model is definitely generating actions and sometimes avoiding collisions, but it never actually completes any tasks. This tells me the model isn't just outputting zeros—it's producing real behavior, just not behavior that solves the tasks. The training loss decreases, but at evaluation time the model fails to generate task-completing actions, which points to a fundamental mismatch between what the model learns during training and what it needs to do at test time.

I'm thinking through the most likely culprits: action normalization could be applied differently between training and inference, the action chunk dimensions might be ordered differently, the camera configuration could have changed, the timestep conventions might not align, or the instruction formatting could be inconsistent. This is a critical issue that needs immediate attention.
```

</details>

  "text": "[severity: critical] 系统性 TSR=0% — 不是训练步数问题，可能是 eval pipeline 配置错误\n\n## 证据\n\n**所有 baseline 在所有 suite 上 TSR=0%**，跨越两代配置和多个 seed：\n\n| 实验 | 配置 | 步数 | Loss | SafeLIBERO TSR |\n|------|------|------|------|---------------|\n| baseline_s1 (v1) | bs=4, 1 GPU | 30K | 0.024 | **0% (全部 4 suites)** |\n| baseline_s2 (v1) | bs=4, 1 GPU | 30K | 0.026 | **0% (Spatial + Object 已查)** |\n| baseline_v2_s1 | bs=128, DDP 2GPU | 5K | 0.015 | **0% (sanity check)** |\n\n关键观察：\n- **CAR 不为零**（20-90% 不等）→ 机器人确实在动，不是输出全零\n- **30K 步（v1）和 5K 步（v2）都是 0%** → \"训练更久\" 大概率无效\n- v2 实际看了更多数据（640K samples vs 120K）但仍然 0%\n- eval 日志有大量 \"Too many contacts\" 警告 → 机器人在做剧烈运动但无法完成任务\n\n## 判断\n\n**D004 的假设已被证伪**：batch_size 不是 TSR=0 的根本原因。问题更可能出在 training-inference 管线的某个环节：\n\n1. **Action normalization 不匹配**：训练用 normalized actions，eval 的 denormalization 不一致\n2. **Action chunk 格式错位**：dim 顺序（pos/rot/gripper）在 train 和 eval 之间不同\n3. **观测预处理差异**：camera view、分辨率、或 image preprocessing 不同\n4. **ODE solver 配置**：推理时积分步数或时间范围与训练预设不同\n5. **Language instruction 格式**：tokenization 不同导致 conditioning 失效\n\n## 建议（按优先级）\n\n1. **立即用 pretrained π₀.₅ 原始权重（不 fine-tune）跑同样的 eval** — 如果原始模型也 TSR=0，确认是 eval pipeline 问题而非训练问题\n2. **对照 openpi 官方 LIBERO eval 配置** — 检查 action normalization stats、action dim、camera config 是否与训练完全一致\n3. **暂缓 resume to 10K（Plan B）** — 在排除 eval 管线问题前，增加训练步数几乎不会改变结果\n4. **geosafe_v2_s1 的 sanity check 也应该跑** — 但鉴于 baseline 都是 0%，geosafe 大概率也是 0%，优先级低于 debug\n\n这是 MVP 的 blocking issue。如果 eval pipeline 有 bug，此前所有 TSR/CAR 数据都不可信。",
  "project": "geosafe_vla"
}

2	  "raw": "诊断完成。以下是完整报告。\n\n---\n\n## 诊断报告：baseline_v2_s1 TSR=0% Root Cause 分析\n\n### 1. 🔴 ROOT CAUSE: 图像旋转 bug\n\n**官方 openpi** (`examples/libero/main.py:115-116`):\n```python\n# IMPORTANT: rotate 180 degrees to match train preprocessing\nimg = np.ascontiguousarray(obs[\"agentview_image\"][::-1, ::-1])\nwrist_img = np.ascontiguousarray(obs[\"robot0_eye_in_hand_image\"][::-1, ::-1])\n```\n`[::-1, ::-1]` = **180° 旋转**（同时翻转 height 和 width 轴）\n\n**我们的 eval 脚本** (`eval_standard_libero.py` & `eval_baseline.py`):\n```python\nimg = obs[\"agentview_image\"][::-1]\nwrist_img = obs[\"robot0_eye_in_hand_image\"][::-1]\n```\n`[::-1]` = **仅垂直翻转**（只翻转 height 轴）\n\n**差异**：缺少水平翻转，等价于图像被左右镜像。模型看到的空间布局完全反了——\"碗在盘子和 ramekin 之间\"变成了镜像位置，机器人向错误方向伸手。\n\n这解释了：\n- **TSR=0%**：方向全错，抓不到目标\n- **CAR 非零 / 机器人在动**：模型确实在产出 action，只是基于错误的视觉输入\n\n### 2. 🟡 次要问题: 缺少 `convert_to_uint8`\n\n官方在 resize 后包了 `image_tools.convert_to_uint8()`，我们没有。`resize_with_pad` 可能返回 float array，不过服务端 `LiberoInputs._parse_image` 有 float→uint8 转换逻辑，所以实际影响有限。\n\n### 3. 🟡 次要问题: 缺少 `np.ascontiguousarray`\n\n官方用 `np.ascontiguousarray()` 确保内存连续，我们没有。翻转后的 view 是非连续的，可能影响序列化效率，但不太会导致功能错误。\n\n### 4. ✅ Norm stats：一致\n\nbase 模型和 finetuned checkpoint 的 `norm_stats.json` **完全相同**。actions 维度 7（pos 3 + rot 3 + gripper 1），数值范围合理。排除 normalization 不匹配。\n\n### 5. ✅ Weight loading：正常\n\nServe 日志无 weight mismatch 警告，模型加载干净。\n\n### 6. ✅ 其他配置：基本一致\n\n- `max_steps=220`、`replan_steps=5`、`resize_size=224`、`seed=7` 均一致\n- `num_steps_wait` 处理方式略有不同（官方在主循环内计数，我们单独循环），但总 action step 预算相同\n- action 构造方式（state concat 顺序、prompt 传递）一致\n\n### 修复方案\n\n在 `eval_standard_libero.py` 和 `eval_baseline.py` 中，将：\n```python\nimg = obs[\"agentview_image\"][::-1]\nwrist_img = obs[\"robot0_eye_in_hand_image\"][::-1]\n```\n改为：\n```python\nimg = np.ascontiguousarray(obs[\"agentview_image\"][::-1, ::-1])\nwrist_img = np.ascontiguousarray(obs[\"robot0_eye_in_hand_image\"][::-1, ::-1])\n```\n同时补上 `convert_to_uint8`：\n```python\nimg_resized = image_tools.convert_to_uint8(\n    image_tools.resize_with_pad(img, args.resize_size, args.resize_size)\n)\nwrist_resized = image_tools.convert_to_uint8(\n    image_tools.resize_with_pad(wrist_img, args.resize_size, args.resize_size)\n)\n```\n\n**预期**：修复后 TSR 应从 0% 恢复到 baseline 水平（pi0.5 LIBERO spatial 官方报告 ~90%+）。"

_source line 3352 · 2026-05-20T12:48:40.763Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Excellent! This is a clear root cause finding. The eval scripts were only doing vertical flip (`[::-1]`) instead of the required 180° rotation (`[::-1, ::-1]`). This means the model was seeing a left-right mirrored image, causing it to reach in the wrong direction every time.

This is significant because:
1. It explains TSR=0% across ALL runs — the bug is in the eval pipeline, not training
2. It means all prior eval results (v1 baselines on SafeLIBERO) are invalid
3. The training itself is likely fine — both baseline and geosafe models learned correctly


4. The fix is straightforward — just add the missing horizontal flip

This is actually great news for the project since it means we don't need to retrain anything, just correct the eval pipeline and run it again. The root cause is clear: the official OpenPI code explicitly requires a 180° rotation to match training preprocessing, but the eval script only applied the vertical flip, leaving images left-right mirrored. I'm confident this is the issue.
```

</details>

Base Model (pi05_base_final) Eval\n\nTSR = 0% (0/20)。**预期结果**——base 模型未在 LIBERO 上 fine-tune，不能完成任务。\n\n### 3. Baseline_v2_s1 行为分析（关键发现）\n\n**模型已学到有意义的行为模式：**\n\n| 阶段 | Step | 行为 |\n|------|------|------|\n| 到达 | 0-40 | EEF 向右前方移动，z 从 1.17→1.05 下降 |\n| 抓取尝试 | ~60 | gripper 从 -1.0→+0.96 (**关闭！**) |\n| 重新定位 | 80-120 | gripper 重新打开，继续调整 |\n| 再次抓取 | 140-180 | gripper +1.0，z 降至 0.91（最低点）|\n| 抬起尝试 | 200+ | z 从 0.91 回升至 1.04 |\n\ngripper 在整个 episode 中 min=-1.02, max=+1.01，**确认模型学会了开合控制**。但精度不够，无法完成完整的 pick-and-place。\n\n### 4. 根因：训练量严重不足\n\n| | baseline_v2_s1 | 官方 openpi |\n|---|---|---|\n| batch_size | 128 | **256** |\n| steps | 5,000 | **30,000** |\n| warmup | 1,500 | **10,000** |\n| 总样本量 | 640K | **7.68M (12x)** |\n| v1 总样本量 | 120K (bs=4×30K) | 7.68M (**64x**) |\n\n- v2 只看了 ~2 遍数据（338K frames × 2 epoch）\n- 官方训练看了 ~23 遍\n\n### 5. 无法下载官方 Fine-tuned Checkpoint\n\n`gs://openpi-assets/checkpoints/pi05_libero` 从此服务器下载极慢（~1 MB/s，预计 3.4h）。gsutil 直连 GCS 被墙。\n\n### 结论\n\n**不是 eval pipeline bug，是训练量问题。** 建议：\n1. 用官方超参训练：`bs=256, steps=30000, warmup=10000`\n2. 用多 GPU 支撑 bs=256（4 卡 H800 足够）\n3. 或至少保证总样本量 >5M"

Base Model (pi05_base_final) Eval\\n\\nTSR = 0% (0/20)。**预期结果**——base 模型未在 LIBERO 上 fine-tune，不能完成任务。\\n\\n### 3. Baseline_v2_s1 行为分析（关键发现）\\n\\n**模型已学到有意义的行为模式：**\\n\\n| 阶段 | Step | 行为 |\\n|------|------|------|\\n| 到达 | 0-40 | EEF 向右前方移动，z 从 1.17→1.05 下降 |\\n| 抓取尝试 | ~60 | gripper 从 -1.0→+0.96 (**关闭！**) |\\n| 重新定位 | 80-120 | gripper 重新打开，继续调整 |\\n| 再次抓取 | 140-180 | gripper +1.0，z 降至 0.91（最低点）|\\n| 抬起尝试 | 200+ | z 从 0.91 回升至 1.04 |\\n\\ngripper 在整个 episode 中 min=-1.02, max=+1.01，**确认模型学会了开合控制**。但精度不够，无法完成完整的 pick-and-place。\\n\\n### 4. 根因：训练量严重不足\\n\\n| | baseline_v2_s1 | 官方 openpi |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| batch_size | 128 | **256** |\\n| steps | 5,000 | **30,000** |\\n| warmup | 1,500 | **10,000** |\\n| 总样本量 | 640K | **7.68M (12x)** |\\n| v1 总样本量 | 120K (bs=4×30K) | 7.68M (**64x**) |\\n\\n- v2 只看了 ~2 遍数据（338K frames × 2 epoch）\\n- 官方训练看了 ~23 遍\\n\\n### 5. 无法下载官方 Fine-tuned Checkpoint\\n\\n`gs://openpi-assets/checkpoints/pi05_libero` 从此服务器下载极慢（~1 MB/s，预计 3.4h）。gsutil 直连 GCS 被墙。\\n\\n### 结论\\n\\n**不是 eval pipeline bug，是训练量问题。** 建议：\\n1. 用官方超参训练：`bs=256, steps=30000, warmup=10000`\\n2. 用多 GPU 支撑 bs=256（4 卡 H800 足够）\\n3. 或至少保证总样本量 >5M\"\n}",

_source line 3374 · 2026-05-20T13:08:28.766Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a very important diagnostic report. Let me analyze:

1. **Eval pipeline is correct** — the worker did a line-by-line comparison with the official openpi LIBERO eval script and confirmed everything matches. This rules out eval pipeline bugs.

2. **Base model (not fine-tuned) also TSR=0%** — this is expected since it wasn't trained on LIBERO.

3. **Baseline_v2_s1 shows meaningful behavior** — the robot is reaching, attempting to grasp (gripper opening/closing), repositioning, and lifting. This confirms the model IS learning, just not enough.
   
4. **The training scale is the bottleneck** — v1 only processed 120K samples across 30K steps with a batch size of 4, while v2 managed 640K samples with a batch size of 128 over 5K steps. The official model trained on 7.68M samples, which is 12 times more than v2 and 64 times more than v1. Both my versions are severely underfed compared to what's needed to reach the reported performance.

To hit that 7.68M sample target, I'd need either 60K steps at batch size 128 (taking about 4.7 days on 2 GPUs) or 30K steps at batch size 256 on 4 GPUs (still around 113 hours wall time). Either way, the timeline is tight given the CoRL deadline and the need for evaluation, ablations, and paper writing.

The real constraint is that I need to run baseline and geosafe in parallel using 4 GPUs total, which takes 2.4 days. Then I'd need to add seed variations and ablations on top of that—potentially another 4.8 days for seeds alone if I can only run one pair at a time, pushing the finish date to late May. The ablations would require even more runs, making this extremely constrained for the CoRL deadline.

Let me reconsider the approach: if I reduce to 30K steps with batch size 128 instead of 256, I'd get 3.84M samples—half the official amount but potentially still viable. That keeps the wall time at 2.4 days for the main pair. The question is whether I should pursue this reduced setup or find a way to overlap the ablations more efficiently.

Actually, looking back at the v1 baselines trained at 30K steps with batch size 4—they only saw 120K samples, which is 5× less than v2's 640K. So v2 is clearly better trained despite the rotation issues. The core problem remains: I need significantly more training data and compute time, which reshapes the entire project timeline. I should flag this assessment with the Director.
```

_source line 3407 · 2026-05-20T14:21:32.403Z_

[System 触发审查]:
训练空窗期（~56h）战略讨论请求。

背景：D005 30K 步训练已启动（bs=128, 2+2 GPU 并行），10K checkpoint ETA 5/21 08:40 UTC。CoRL paper DDL 5/29。已向 Manager 申请云 GPU。

请分析以下问题：

## 1. Claim 策略
30K 步我们用 bs=128（总样本 3.84M），官方是 bs=256（7.68M），只有官方的 50%。这意味着：
- 如果 TSR 达到官方水平 → 强 claim，证明我们的训练效率与原始一致
- 如果 TSR 低于官方但 >0% → 可以 claim "SDF loss 不严重影响任务性能"，但需要 caveat
- 如果 TSR 远低于官方 → 需要分析是 bs 不够还是 SDF loss 干扰

不同 TSR 水平下，我们的 core claim（SDF internalization improves CAR without sacrificing TSR）需要怎么调整 framing？

## 2. Ablation 优先级
时间只够 2 个 ablation（假设没有云 GPU）。从以下候选中选 2 个对论文 story 最关键的：
- A1: No collision loss (λ_col=0) — 证明 collision shaping 的必要性
- A2: No backbone gradient (detach) — 证明 gradient 回传的必要性
- A3: Constant w(t)=1 — 证明 noise-aware weighting 的必要性
- A4: High collision weight (λ_col=0.1) — 证明当前权重的合理性

## 3. 10K checkpoint 利用策略
如果 10K 步 eval 显示 TSR>0% 但不高（比如 20-40%），这个中间结果在论文中能怎么用？
- 作为 "learning curve" 图？
- 作为 "efficiency analysis"？
- 还是只作为内部 MVP 信号，不放进论文？

## 4. 最小可行论文配置
如果只有本地 4 GPU、CoRL 5/29 DDL，最小能支撑投稿的实验配置是什么？

```text
Let me review the updated ROADMAP and also address the strategic discussion questions from the Director.

ROADMAP Review:
1. D005 training plan is sound — 30K steps, bs=128, 2+2 GPU parallel, ~56h
2. 10K checkpoint eval at 5/21 08:40 — good intermediate checkpoint
3. Timeline is tight but achievable for minimal paper
4. Cloud GPU request is appropriate

Let me also address the Director's strategic questions:

## 1. Claim Strategy

The key issue: bs=128 × 30K = 3.84M samples vs official bs=256 × 30K = 7.68M (50% of official).


Now I'm thinking through the different TSR outcome scenarios and how they shape the paper's narrative. If TSR lands near official levels, that's a strong position—I can argue that maintaining comparable performance on half the samples actually strengthens the case for the SDF loss approach. If TSR drops to moderate levels, the story shifts toward emphasizing the CAR gains as the primary contribution while acknowledging the performance trade-off. If TSR ends up low, I'll need to reconsider the framing entirely.

The crucial insight is that what matters for the paper is comparing GeoSafe against the vanilla baseline under identical training budgets. Even if both show low TSR, if GeoSafe achieves higher CAR, the core contribution still holds—it just becomes a weaker overall paper. The key ablation I absolutely need is removing the collision loss entirely to verify it's actually driving the CAR improvements, rather than some other aspect of the approach.

Beyond that, detaching the SDF head from the backbone is the critical test of whether safety truly gets internalized into the action generation weights or if the SDF head is just functioning as a standalone auxiliary predictor. These two ablations directly validate the paper's central claims, while the others are secondary refinements.

For the 10K checkpoint, if it shows positive transfer, I should include a learning curve plotting both TSR and CAR across checkpoints to visualize how GeoSafe's training dynamics compare to the baseline—this would reveal whether the SDF loss accelerates convergence or enables better sample efficiency, which is genuinely insightful for understanding the approach.

Given the GPU and time constraints, I need to run the two main experiments (baseline and GeoSafe at 30K), then execute both ablations in parallel once the main pair finishes around 5/22, completing everything by 5/24 to leave time for results compilation and writing before the 5/29 deadline.

To make this work, I'm cutting down to a single seed and moving A3/A4 ablations to the appendix as future work, while dropping the supplementary ablations entirely. The core paper will have three comparison tables—one showing our method against baselines, another with just the two key ablations, and a latency analysis—plus the method overview and weight curve visualizations. It's minimal but should be solid if the numbers are strong enough for CoRL.

Before I post this to the Director, I need to double-check the roadmap itself. The experiment labels reference v2 runs, but those were the 5K-step versions—I should be using v3 instead. The timeline looks feasible, though the seed 2 launch on 5/23 only works if the cloud GPU arrives by then. Let me verify these details and compile the response.
```

GeoSafe v3 crashed immediately due to a DDP port conflict — both runs tried to use port 29500. The fix is simple: add `--master_port 29501` to the geosafe run. 

This is a straightforward operational issue, not a research concern. The baseline is running fine. The geosafe run just needs to be restarted with a different port.

Key concern: geosafe_v3_s1 crashed at startup and GPU 2,3 are now idle. This is wasting GPU time. I should flag this as urgent but it's a simple fix.


Checking the GPU allocation against the red line thresholds — baseline_v3_s1 is on GPU 0,1 and geosafe_v3_s1 was on GPU 2,3, so both stay within the 4-GPU boundary. The idle GPUs aren't a red line violation since the process crashed rather than being allocated but unused by design.
```

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_4/projects/geosafe_vla/ROADMAP.md\", \"content\": \"# geosafe_vla — Research Dashboard\\n\\n> 最后更新: 2026-05-20 14:25 UTC | 更新原因: 战略决策落地：claim 策略 + ablation A1/A2 优先 + learning curve + 最小可行论文配置 + 修正 D005 关联实验\\n\\n## 核心方向\\n将 SDF 碰撞几何通过 collision-shaping loss 在训练时内化到 flow-matching VLA (π₀.₅) 权重中，推理时丢弃 SDF head，零额外开销生成安全动作。\\n\\n## 投稿目标\\n**CoRL 2026** — Abstract: 2026-05-26 (6天) | Paper: 2026-05-29 (9天)\\n\\n## 当前阶段：MVP 验证 — D005 30K 步训练运行中\\n\\n### 关键发现\\n- Eval 图像旋转 bug 已修复，pipeline 经逐行 diff 确认正确\\n- D004 TSR=0% 根因：训练量不足（5K 步 640K 样本 vs 官方 30K 步 7.68M）\\n- geosafe_v3_s1 因 DDP 端口冲突崩溃，已用 --master_port 29501 重启（晚 ~33min）\\n\\n### D005 训练状态（4/4 GPU）\\n| Run | GPUs | Steps | warmup | port | ETA |\\n|-----|------|-------|--------|------|-----|\\n| baseline_v3_s1 | 0,1 | 30K | 10000 | 29500 | ~5/22 22:30 UTC |\\n| geosafe_v3_s1 | 2,3 | 30K | 10000 | 29501 | ~5/22 23:00 UTC |\\n\\n### MVP 判定\\n- 10K 步 TSR>0% → 早期正向信号（ETA 5/21 ~08:40 UTC）\\n- Core claim 成立条件：baseline 与 GeoSafe TSR 差距 ≤5pp 且 GeoSafe CAR 显著高于 baseline\\n\\n### Claim 策略（Reviewer 审定）\\n论文 claim 取决于 GeoSafe vs baseline 公平对比，不取决于是否达到官方 TSR 水平。\\n\\n### CoRL Timeline（最小可行配置）\\n| 日期 | 事件 |\\n|------|------|\\n| 5/21 ~08:40 UTC | 10K checkpoint eval → 早期 MVP 信号 |\\n| 5/22 ~22:30 UTC | 30K 训练完成 → full eval → MVP 判定 |\\n| 5/23 | Ablation A1 (no collision loss) + A2 (no backbone grad) 启动，2+2 GPU 并行 |\\n| 5/25 凌晨 | Ablation 完成 → eval |\\n| **5/26** | **Abstract 提交**（1 seed + 2 ablation 数据） |\\n| 5/26-29 | 结果填入 + LaTeX 排版 + 终稿打磨 |\\n| **5/29** | **Paper 提交** |\\n\\n如获云 GPU → 5/23 同时启动 seed 2，增强统计显著性。\\n\\n### Ablation 优先级（Reviewer 审定）\\n1. **A1: No collision loss** (λ_col=0) — 验证 collision shaping 必要性\\n2. **A2: No backbone gradient** (detach) — 验证 internalization thesis（最关键）\\n3. A3/A4 → future work（时间不够）\\n\\n### Learning Curve 计划\\n5K/10K/15K/20K/25K/30K 每个 checkpoint eval → Fig 3 TSR+CAR 曲线\\n\\n## 论文准备\\n- [x] 全部 7 section draft + holistic review 修复\\n- [x] Fig 1 + Fig 2\\n- [x] Literature map 16 篇\\n- [ ] Ablation 训练脚本准备中\\n- [ ] Learning curve eval 脚本准备中\\n- [ ] 实验数据填入 → Results 叙述\\n- [ ] LaTeX 排版\\n\\n## GPU 预算\\nwestd-18751: 4× RTX PRO 6000 96GB，4/4 满载\\n- D005 主训练：~224 GPU-h（4 GPU × 56h）\\n- Ablation A1+A2：~224 GPU-h\\n- Eval + learning curve：~20 GPU-h\\n- 总需求：~468 GPU-h vs 可用 ~816 → 充裕\\n- 云 GPU 申请：已提交 Manager，等回复\\n\\n## 关键决策日志\\n\\n| ID | 日期 | 摘要 | 关联实验 |\\n|----|------|------|----------|\\n| D005 | 2026-05-20 | D005: 30K 步重训 — bs=128 DDP 2GPU 并行 baseline+geosaf | baseline_v2_s1, geosafe_v2_s1 |\\n| D004 | 2026-05-20 | D004: 快速 MVP — 2-GPU 并行, bs=128, 5K 步, baseline+Ge | baseline_s1, geosafe_s1 |\\n| D003 | 2026-05-20 | D003: 重训策略 — DDP 4 GPU, 先 batch_size=64 验证 TSR>0,  | baseline_s1, baseline_s2, geosafe_s1 |\\n| D002 | 2026-05-19 | GeoSafe MVP 训练必须启用 sdf_grad_to_backbone（梯度回传 backb | — |\\n| D001 | 2026-05-19 | MVP baseline 和 treatment 均使用 full fine-tuning（非 Lo | baseline_s1, baseline_s2, baseline_s3 |\\n\\n\\n\\n\", \"lines\": 81}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {}}"}

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\documentclass{article}\\n\\\\usepackage{corl_2026}\\n\\\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,graphicx,booktabs,xcolor,hyperref,microtype}\\n\\\\usepackage[normalem]{ulem}\\n\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\placeholder}[1]{\\\\textcolor{red}{[#1]}}\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\ours}{GeoSafe}\\n\\n\\\\title{GeoSafe: Internalizing Collision Geometry into Flow-Matching VLA Weights for Zero-Overhead Safe Manipulation}\\n\\n\\\\author{Anonymous}\\n\\n\\\\begin{document}\\n\\\\maketitle\\n\\n\\\\begin{abstract}\\nVision-Language-Action (VLA) models have advanced robot manipulation toward general-purpose deployment, yet collision safety remains an unresolved challenge for real-world use. Most existing safety methods operate at inference time, adding computational overhead; training-time alternatives based on reinforcement learning reduce violations but lack geometric grounding for precise spatial collision reasoning.\\n\\nWe propose \\\\ours{}, a training-time approach that embeds collision awareness directly into flow-matching VLA weights. During fine-tuning, a lightweight auxiliary head predicts per-timestep Signed Distance Field (SDF) values from the model's internal representations, and a collision-shaping loss penalizes trajectories that violate a safety margin. A noise-aware weighting function $w(t)$ suppresses this penalty at high noise levels where geometric predictions are unreliable, activating it only in the low-noise regime where action predictions carry meaningful spatial structure. After training, the SDF head is discarded entirely: the model runs through the standard inference pipeline with zero additional latency, zero additional memory, and no access to scene geometry.\\n\\nWe evaluate \\\\ours{} on the SafeLIBERO benchmark (32 collision scenarios across 4 task suites), measuring Task Success Rate (TSR) and Collision Avoidance Rate (CAR). \\\\ours{} achieves \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% TSR and \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% CAR, compared to \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% TSR / \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% CAR for vanilla $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ and \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% TSR / \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% CAR for the strongest inference-time baseline. Our approach establishes a connection between guidance distillation in image generation and safety internalization in robot control, providing a ``train-with, infer-without'' framework for embedding geometric safety constraints into generative action policies.\\n\\\\end{abstract}\\n\\n%=============================================================================\\n\\\\section{Introduction}\\n%=============================================================================\\n\\nVision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as a powerful paradigm for general-purpose robot manipulation. $\\\\pi_0$ \\\\citep{black2024pi0} demonstrated that a flow-matching architecture built on a pretrained vision-language model can master diverse manipulation tasks across multiple robot platforms, and its successor $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ \\\\citep{black2025pi05} extended this capability to open-world generalization through co-training on heterogeneous data. These models generate smooth, language-conditioned action trajectories by learning velocity fields that transport noise distributions to action distributions, enabling flexible deployment without task-specific engineering. Yet a critical gap separates laboratory success from real-world deployment: collision safety. When a robot executes a manipulation task in a cluttered environment, unintended contact with surrounding objects or the workspace boundary can damage both the robot and the environment, and create safety hazards for nearby humans. {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\begin{equation}\\nx_t = (1 - t) \\\\, a + t \\\\, \\\\epsilon,\\n\\\\end{equation}\\nwhere $t = 0$ corresponds to the clean action and $t = 1$ to pure noise. The conditional velocity field target is $u_t = \\\\epsilon - a$, and the flow-matching objective minimizes\\n\\\\begin{equation}\\n\\\\mathcal{L}_{\\\\mathrm{FM}} = \\\\mathbb{E}_{t, a, \\\\epsilon} \\\\left[ \\\\left\\\\| v_\\\\theta(x_t, o, t) - (\\\\epsilon - a) \\\\right\\\\|^2 \\\\right].\\n\\\\end{equation}\\nAt inference, the model generates actions by integrating the learned velocity field from $t = 1$ (noise) to $t = 0$ (data) using an ODE solver.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Safety as collision avoidance.} We formalize safety through the signed distance field between the robot's end-effector and scene obstacles. For a robot configuration $q$ in a scene with obstacle set $\\\\mathcal{O}$, the signed distance $s(q) = \\\\min_{o \\\\in \\\\mathcal{O}} \\\\mathrm{SDF}(q, o)$ is positive when the end-effector is separated from all obstacles and negative when penetration occurs. A trajectory is considered safe if $s(q_t) \\\\geq \\\\delta$ for all timesteps, where $\\\\delta > 0$ is a safety margin. Our goal is to train a policy that intrinsically generates trajectories satisfying this constraint, without requiring runtime collision checking or trajectory filtering.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Core insight.} Rather than imposing safety through constrained optimization at inference (which adds latency and may degrade task performance), we shape the flow-matching training objective so that the learned velocity field steers action trajectories away from collision regions. The SDF auxiliary head acts as a geometric probe during training: it reads the model's internal representation and provides a differentiable safety signal. When gradients from the collision-shaping loss propagate back through this probe into the action generation backbone, the model learns to associate its internal representations with collision-free trajectories.\\n\\n%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n\\\\subsection{SDF Auxiliary Head}\\n\\\\label{sec:sdf_head}\\n%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Architecture.} The SDF head is a two-layer multilayer perceptron (MLP) that maps the action expert's internal features to per-timestep signed distance predictions. In $\\\\pi_{0.5}$, the action expert is a Gemma-300M transformer that processes noisy action tokens alongside vision-language context. Let $f_t \\\\in \\\\mathbb{R}^{B \\\\times H \\\\times D}$ denote the action expert's output features (with $D = 1024$ for the Gemma-300M variant), which are normally projected to action-space dimension $D_a = 32$ by a linear output layer. We attach the SDF head to these features via a forward hook on the output projection layer, requiring no modification to the base model's source code. The head is defined as\\n\\\\begin{equation}\\n\\\\hat{s} = W_2 \\\\, \\\\mathrm{ReLU}(W_1 \\\\, f_t + b_1) + b_2,\\n\\\\end{equation}\\nwhere $W_1 \\\\in \\\\mathbb{R}^{256 \\\\times 1024}$, $W_2 \\\\in \\\\mathbb{R}^{1 \\\\times 256}$, and $\\\\hat{s} \\\\in \\\\mathbb{R}^{B \\\\times H}$ gives the predicted minimum signed distance for each timestep in the action chunk. The head contains approximately 263K parameters, which is negligible compared to the 300M-parameter action expert.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Ground-truth SDF computation.} We precompute per-timestep signed distance labels offline from the demonstration dataset using MuJoCo's analytical geometry engine. For each demonstration timestep, we replay the recorded robot state and compute the minimum signed distance between end-effector collision geometries (gripper fingers and the last two arm links) and all scene object geometries, using \\\\texttt{mj\\\\_geomDistance} for exact signed distance queries. Objects currently grasped by the robot are excluded from distance computation to avoid penalizing intentional contact.\\\\footnote{Grasp is detected when the gripper aperture falls below 35\\\\,mm and the minimum finger-to-object distance is less than 5\\\\,mm. See Appendix for implementation details.}\\n\\nThe \\n\\n... [截断: 输出过长，省略 23604 字符] ...\\n\\nroaches that impose safety at inference time through guided sampling \\\\citep{song2026omniguide}, post-hoc trajectory correction \\\\citep{hu2025aegis}, or constrained reinforcement learning \\\\citep{zhang2025safevla}, \\\\ours{} produces a model whose standard forward pass generates inherently collision-aware actions with zero additional latency, memory, or access to scene geometry at deployment.\\n\\nOur work makes three contributions. First, we showed that SDF collision geometry can be internalized into the velocity field of a flow-matching VLA during training, and that the SDF auxiliary head used for this purpose can be discarded entirely at inference. The resulting model runs through the unmodified $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ pipeline, yet its learned velocity field routes action trajectories away from collision boundaries. Second, we introduced a noise-aware collision-shaping loss whose time-dependent weight $w(t) = \\\\sigma(\\\\alpha(t_0 - t))$ activates the collision penalty only in the low-noise regime where the model's geometric predictions carry reliable spatial information. Third, we evaluated \\\\ours{} on SafeLIBERO (32 collision scenarios across 4 task suites) and four standard LIBERO suites, demonstrating \\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\% collision avoidance rate (compared to \\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\% for the vanilla $\\\\pi_{0.5}$) while maintaining \\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\% task success rate. This result validates a conceptual bridge between guidance distillation in image generation and safety internalization in VLA: the ``train-with, infer-without'' principle extends to geometric safety constraints for robot action generation.\\n\\nSeveral directions remain open. The current approach relies on SDF labels precomputed from simulator geometry; extending \\\\ours{} to real-world settings will require neural SDF estimation from depth observations or point clouds. Evaluating \\\\ours{} on additional VLA backbones beyond $\\\\pi_{0.5}$, such as Octo and RT-2, would establish the generality of the internalization principle. Finally, real-world deployment with truly novel object categories and deformable or transparent obstacles remains an important validation step.\\n\\n\\\\subsection*{Limitations}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Single benchmark.} All experiments were conducted on LIBERO and SafeLIBERO in the MuJoCo simulator. {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"38:\\\\item We present the first method that internalizes SDF collision geometry into VLA flow-matching weights during training, producing a model that generates inherently collision-aware actions with zero inference overhead. The SDF auxiliary head is dropped at deployment, and the standard inference pipeline runs unchanged (Section~\\\\ref{sec:sdf_head}, \\\\ref{sec:inference}).\\n39:\\\\item We propose a noise-aware collision-shaping loss with a time-dependent weight function $w(t)$ that adapts to the flow-matching noise schedule, activating collision penalties only in the low-noise regime where the model's geometric predictions carry reliable spatial information. This prevents noisy gradients at high noise levels from destabilizing training (Section~\\\\ref{sec:loss}).\\n40:\\\\item Comprehensive evaluation on SafeLIBERO benchmark comparing with inference-time safety method (AEGIS) and controlled baseline (vanilla $\\\\pi_{0.5}$), demonstrating \\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\% improvement in collision avoidance with negligible task performance degradation (Section~\\\\ref{sec:experiments}).\\n41:\\\\item We establish a conceptual bridge between guidance distillation in image generation and safety internalization in VLA, providing a ``train-with, infer-without'' framework for embedding geometric safety constraints into generative robot policies (Section~\\\\ref{sec:inference}).\\n214:Table~\\\\ref{tab:main} presents results on SafeLIBERO and standard LIBERO.\\n217:\\\\caption{Safety Score ($\\\\mathrm{SS} = \\\\mathrm{TSR} \\\\times \\\\mathrm{CAR}$) on SafeLIBERO (4 suites) and Task Success Rate on standard LIBERO-Spatial. Results report seed=1 with 5 eval trials per task. Best results in \\\\textbf{bold}, second-best \\\\uline{underlined}. Per-suite TSR and CAR breakdowns are provided in Appendix Table~\\\\ref{tab:full_results}.}\\n241:We conduct four ablation experiments to isolate the contribution of each design choice. All ablations use the same training setup as the full \\\\ours{} model except for the specified modification. Results are summarized in Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}.\\n278:\\\\paragraph{Qualitative comparison with OmniGuide.} OmniGuide is an inference-time guidance method that computes differentiable SDF-based energy fields and backpropagates through differentiable kinematics to steer flow-matching denoising steps toward collision-free trajectories. We exclude OmniGuide from Table~\\\\ref{tab:main} because its published evaluation uses the RoboCasa environment rather than SafeLIBERO, precluding direct numerical comparison on our benchmark. Conceptually, OmniGuide and \\\\ours{} represent two ends of the internalization spectrum: OmniGuide applies geometric reasoning entirely at inference time, while \\\\ours{} absorbs it into the model weights during training.\\n280:\\\\paragraph{Inference latency comparison.} Table~\\\\ref{tab:latency} compares per-action-chunk inference latency across methods.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}

```text
Let me compile my findings from the LaTeX source review:

## 1. Page Count Analysis

From the log: 17 pages total, distributed as:
- Pages 1-10: Main paper (Abstract + Intro + Related Work + Method + Experiments + Conclusion + Limitations + Broader Impact)
- Page 11: Bibliography (main.bbl)
- Pages 12-17: Appendix (6 pages)

CoRL typically allows 8 pages + references + unlimited appendix. So the main content is pages 1-10 = 10 pages. That's 2 pages over the limit.


Looking at the page breaks more carefully, the main content spans pages 1-10, references take page 11, and the appendix fills pages 12-17. For CoRL 2026, the standard is 8 pages for main content plus unlimited references and appendix, which means we're currently 2 pages over the allowed limit.

Now I'm checking the compilation log to see what issues might be contributing to the page count. There are no errors, just some harmless float specifier warnings in the appendix and two underfull vbox warnings on pages 2 and 14 that suggest excessive whitespace. The good news is there are no undefined references or missing citations. I'm also checking whether any figures are actually being included in the document.

The grep search shows no `\includegraphics` commands in the main file, which means the two key figures—the method overview and the weight curves visualization—aren't being rendered in the PDF at all. This is a critical gap since these visuals are essential for understanding the approach. I should also verify that the 17 bibliography entries match up with the 15 citations used throughout the paper.

Looking at the reference list, I notice CoFreeVLA is mentioned in the text with a year but isn't formally cited with `\citep`, which is another issue to flag. I'm also checking which section labels are defined to see if all cross-references are properly set up.

Now I'm going through the defined labels across sections and tables, then comparing them against what's actually referenced in the document. I see that `tab:full_results` is cited in Table 1's caption but I need to confirm it's actually defined in the appendix. I'm also counting the placeholder values scattered throughout the abstract, introduction, and tables to get a sense of how many need to be filled in before submission.

Looking at the LaTeX setup, the `ulem` package is loaded for underlining the second-best results in Table 1, which is fine. The bigger issue is that there are no `\includegraphics` commands anywhere, so figures aren't being included at all. The table placement options are reasonable, though appendix tables using `[h]` will get converted to `[ht]` by LaTeX, which doesn't cause problems.

The critical problems are that the main paper is likely exceeding the typical 8-9 page CoRL limit with 10 pages of content before references, and the figures mentioned in the text aren't actually being rendered in the PDF. There's also a missing citation for CoFreeVLA—it's referenced by name with "(2026)" but lacks a proper `\citep` command and bibliography entry. Table 2 needs cleanup since two of the four ablation rows (the constant weight and high collision weight variants) won't have results and should be removed. The underfull vbox warning on page 2 suggests a potential layout issue worth investigating, and while there are 13 float specifier warnings from the appendix, those are harmless. Around 54 placeholder values still need to be filled in once experiments run, which is expected at this stage.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01W7HWRXGWqDMsaWFoWzAYh7`</summary>

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"69:In traditional planning, CSSDF-Net \\\\citep{chen2026cssdf} models continuous differentiable SDF directly in configuration space for collision-free trajectory optimization, and NGDF \\\\citep{weng2023ngdf} represents grasps as neural distance fields whose gradients enable joint collision avoidance and trajectory smoothness, with 63\\\\% higher execution success than baselines.\\n73:\\\\ours{} specializes the ``train-with, infer-without'' approach to collision avoidance: rather than general spatial alignment, we internalize configuration-space SDF into the policy network through an auxiliary loss that directly shapes flow-matching velocity fields away from obstacle boundaries.\\n82:\\\\ours{} occupies a unique position at the intersection of training-time safety internalization and zero-overhead inference: it is the only approach that combines collision-specific geometric supervision (configuration-space SDF) with full internalization into VLA weights, producing inherently safe actions without runtime safety computation, post-hoc correction, or out-of-distribution drift.\\n107:\\\\paragraph{Safety as collision avoidance.} We formalize safety through the signed distance field between the robot's end-effector and scene obstacles. For a robot configuration $q$ in a scene with obstacle set $\\\\mathcal{O}$, the signed distance $s(q) = \\\\min_{o \\\\in \\\\mathcal{O}} \\\\mathrm{SDF}(q, o)$ is positive when the end-effector is separated from all obstacles and negative when penetration occurs. A trajectory is considered safe if $s(q_t) \\\\geq \\\\delta$ for all timesteps, where $\\\\delta > 0$ is a safety margin. Our goal is to train a policy that intrinsically generates trajectories satisfying this constraint, without requiring runtime collision checking or trajectory filtering.\\n167:\\\\paragraph{Optimization.} We use the AdamW optimizer with the same hyperparameters as the base $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ configuration. The SDF head parameters are added to the same optimizer with shared learning rate and weight decay. Gradients from all parameters (base model and SDF head jointly) are clipped by global norm with $\\\\mathrm{max\\\\_norm} = 1.0$. For the 5,000-step training runs, we use a linear warmup of 1,500 steps. Training uses PyTorch Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) across multiple GPUs, with gradient averaging for both the base model and SDF head parameters. The per-GPU batch size is 64, yielding an effective batch size of 128 on 2 GPUs.\\n197:\\\\paragraph{Base model and training.} We build on $\\\\pi_{0.5}$, the open-source flow-matching VLA released by Physical Intelligence via the openpi codebase. All experiments use full fine-tuning of the entire model (not LoRA adapters), as the available GPU memory permits this and full fine-tuning provides a stronger baseline for isolating the effect of collision shaping. Training runs for 5,000 gradient steps on two NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96\\\\,GB VRAM each) using PyTorch Distributed Data Parallel (DDP). The per-GPU batch size is 64, yielding an effective batch size of 128. We use AdamW with a linear warmup of 1,500 steps to a peak learning rate of $5 \\\\times 10^{-5}$, followed by cosine decay. Gradients from all parameters (base model and SDF head jointly) are clipped by global norm with $\\\\mathrm{max\\\\_norm} = 1.0$. The vanilla $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ model is trained with the identical configuration but without the SDF auxiliary head or collision-shaping loss.\\n199:\\\\paragraph{\\\\ours{} configuration.} The SDF auxiliary head is a two-layer MLP ($1024 \\\\to 256 \\\\to 1$, approximately 263K parameters) attached to the action expert's output features via a forward hook. The total training loss combines the standard flow-matching objective with two auxiliary terms weighted by $\\\\lambda_{\\\\mathrm{SDF}} = 0.1$ and $\\\\lambda_{\\\\mathrm{col}} = 0.01$. The collision penalty uses a safety margin $\\\\delta = 2$\\\\,cm and is gated by a noise-aware sigmoid weight $w(t) = \\\\sigma(\\\\alpha(t_0 - t))$ with transition center $t_0 = 0.5$ and sharpness $\\\\alpha = 10$. Gradients from both SDF losses propagate through the shared backbone features (\\\\texttt{sdf\\\\_grad\\\\_to\\\\_backbone = True}), which is essential for the collision-shaping signal to influence action generation.\\n205:\\\\paragraph{Metrics.} We report three metrics. Task Success Rate (TSR) measures the fraction of episodes in which the robot completes the instructed task. {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\documentclass{article}\\n\\\\usepackage{corl_2026}\\n\\\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,graphicx,booktabs,xcolor,hyperref,microtype}\\n\\\\usepackage[normalem]{ulem}\\n\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\placeholder}[1]{\\\\textcolor{red}{[#1]}}\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\ours}{GeoSafe}\\n\\n\\\\title{GeoSafe: Internalizing Collision Geometry into Flow-Matching VLA Weights for Zero-Overhead Safe Manipulation}\\n\\n\\\\author{Anonymous}\\n\\n\\\\begin{document}\\n\\\\maketitle\\n\\n\\\\begin{abstract}\\nVision-Language-Action (VLA) models have advanced robot manipulation toward general-purpose deployment, yet collision safety remains an unresolved challenge for real-world use. Most existing safety methods operate at inference time, adding computational overhead; training-time alternatives based on reinforcement learning reduce violations but lack geometric grounding for precise spatial collision reasoning.\\n\\nWe propose \\\\ours{}, a training-time approach that embeds collision awareness directly into flow-matching VLA weights. During fine-tuning, a lightweight auxiliary head predicts per-timestep Signed Distance Field (SDF) values from the model's internal representations, and a collision-shaping loss penalizes trajectories that violate a safety margin. A noise-aware weighting function $w(t)$ suppresses this penalty at high noise levels where geometric predictions are unreliable, activating it only in the low-noise regime where action predictions carry meaningful spatial structure. After training, the SDF head is discarded entirely: the model runs through the standard inference pipeline with zero additional latency, zero additional memory, and no access to scene geometry.\\n\\nWe evaluate \\\\ours{} on the SafeLIBERO benchmark (32 collision scenarios across 4 task suites), measuring Task Success Rate (TSR) and Collision Avoidance Rate (CAR). \\\\ours{} achieves \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% TSR and \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% CAR, compared to \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% TSR / \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% CAR for vanilla $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ and \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% TSR / \\\\placeholder{XX.X}\\\\% CAR for the strongest inference-time baseline. Our approach establishes a connection between guidance distillation in image generation and safety internalization in robot control, providing a ``train-with, infer-without'' framework for embedding geometric safety constraints into generative action policies.\\n\\\\end{abstract}\\n\\n%=============================================================================\\n\\\\section{Introduction}\\n%=============================================================================\\n\\nVision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as a powerful paradigm for general-purpose robot manipulation. $\\\\pi_0$ \\\\citep{black2024pi0} demonstrated that a flow-matching architecture built on a pretrained vision-language model can master diverse manipulation tasks across multiple robot platforms, and its successor $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ \\\\citep{black2025pi05} extended this capability to open-world generalization through co-training on heterogeneous data. These models generate smooth, language-conditioned action trajectories by learning velocity fields that transport noise distributions to action distributions, enabling flexible deployment without task-specific engineering. Yet a critical gap separates laboratory success from real-world deployment: collision safety. When a robot executes a manipulation task in a cluttered environment, unintended contact with surrounding objects or the workspace boundary can damage both the robot and the environment, and create safety hazards for nearby humans. The problem is especially acute for VLA models, which generate entire action chunks in a single forward pass without explicit geometric reasoning about obstacle proximity.\\n\\nExisting approaches to VLA collision safety operate primarily at inference time, introducing computational overhead that conflicts with the real-time demands of robot control. OmniGuide \\\\citep{song2026omniguide} applies differentiable Signed Distance Field (SDF) energy to guide flow-matching VLAs through backpropagation into the denoising loop, improving collision avoidance rates from 7.0\\\\% to 93.5\\\\% on RoboCasa but at approximately $2\\\\times$ inference latency. AEGIS/VLSA \\\\citep{hu2025aegis} projects VLA actions into control barrier function (CBF) certified safe regions as a post-hoc correction layer, achieving the strongest results on SafeLIBERO (68.13\\\\% task success rate, 77.85\\\\% collision avoidance rate on $\\\\pi_{0.5}$). The AEGIS authors, however, explicitly acknowledge that such corrections ``may adjust the trajectory to unfamiliar regions that are statistically rare or absent in the training data of the base VLA model,'' leading to erratic downstream behavior. SafeVLA \\\\citep{zhang2025safevla} takes a training-time approach through constrained reinforcement learning, reducing cumulative safety cost by 83.58\\\\%, but relies on abstract cost functions without geometric grounding for spatial collision reasoning. SafeDiffuser \\\\citep{xiao2025safediffuser} embeds CBF invariance directly into the diffusion denoising procedure, but retains the constraint computation at inference. A fundamental tension runs through these methods: inference-time safety interventions add latency and risk pushing trajectories out of distribution, while training-time approaches based on reinforcement learning lack the geometric structure to reason precisely about collision boundaries.\\n\\nWe draw on an observation from image generation: guidance distillation \\\\citep{meng2023distillation} has shown that inference-time guidance signals can be internalized into model weights during training, producing high-fidelity outputs without runtime guidance computation. We transfer this principle to VLA safety. Instead of distilling classifier-free guidance for image quality, we internalize SDF collision geometry into flow-matching velocity fields for safe action generation. The flow-matching training process provides a natural mechanism for this internalization. Each training sample is associated with a noise level $t \\\\in [0, 1]$ that interpolates between the clean action trajectory and pure noise. At low noise levels, the model's internal representations encode meaningful geometric structure about the predicted trajectory. An SDF auxiliary head, attached to these representations, provides a differentiable signal that measures proximity to collision boundaries. By penalizing collision-prone predictions through this head and weighting the penalty with a noise-dependent function that activates only when geometric predictions are reliable, we shape the learned velocity field to route around obstacles without corrupting the training signal at high noise levels.\\n\\nWe propose \\\\ours{}, a training-time method that embeds collision awareness into a flow-matching VLA through SDF auxiliary supervision and a noise-aware collision-shaping loss. A lightweight two-layer network is attached to the action expert's internal features in $\\\\pi_{0.5}$, predicting per-timestep minimum signed distances between the robot's end-effector and scene obstacles. Ground-truth SDF labels are precomputed offline from the simulation physics engine. The collision-shaping loss penalizes predicted trajectories that violate a safety margin $\\\\delta$, weighted by a sigmoid function $w(t) = \\\\sigma(\\\\alpha(t_0 - t))$ that suppresses the penalty at high noise levels and activates it in the low-noise regime where geometric structure is meaningful. Both the SDF regression loss and the collision penalty propagate gradients through the shared backbone, directly modifying the velocity field that governs action generation. At inference time, the SDF head is discarded entirely: the trained model runs through the standard $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ pipeline with zero additional latency, zero additional memory, and no access to scene geometry. Safety becomes an intrinsic property of the modified weights, not an external constraint imposed at runtime.\\n\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.06}}"}

SafeVLA \\\\\\\\citep{zhang2025safevla} takes a training-time approach through constrained reinforcement learning, reducing cumulative safety cost by 83.58\\\\\\\\%, but relies on abstract cost functions without geometric grounding for spatial collision reasoning. SafeDiffuser \\\\\\\\citep{xiao2025safediffuser} embeds CBF invariance directly into the diffusion denoising procedure, but retains the constraint computation at inference. A fundamental tension runs through these methods: inference-time safety interventions add latency and risk pushing trajectories out of distribution, while training-time approaches based on reinforcement learning lack the geometric structure to reason precisely about collision boundaries.\\\\n\\\\nWe draw on an observation from image generation: guidance distillation \\\\\\\\citep{meng2023distillation} has shown that inference-time guidance signals can be internalized into model weights during training, producing high-fidelity outputs without runtime guidance computation. We transfer this principle to VLA safety. Instead of distilling classifier-free guidance for image quality, we internalize SDF collision geometry into flow-matching velocity fields for safe action generation. The flow-matching training process provides a natural mechanism for this internalization. Each training sample is associated with a noise level $t \\\\\\\\in [0, 1]$ that interpolates between the clean action trajectory and pure noise. At low noise levels, the model's internal representations encode meaningful geometric structure about the predicted trajectory. An SDF auxiliary head, attached to these representations, provides a differentiable signal that measures proximity to collision boundaries. By penalizing collision-prone predictions through this head and weighting the penalty with a noise-dependent function that activates only when geometric predictions are reliable, we shape the learned velocity field to route around obstacles without corrupting the training signal at high noise levels.\\\\n\\\\nWe propose \\\\\\\\ours{}, a training-time method that embeds collision awareness into a flow-matching VLA through SDF auxiliary supervision and a noise-aware collision-shaping loss. Ground-truth SDF labels are precomputed offline from the simulation physics engine. Both the SDF regression loss and the collision penalty propagate gradients through the shared backbone, directly modifying the velocity field that governs action generation. At inference time, the SDF head is discarded entirely: the trained model runs through the standard $\\\\\\\\pi_{0.5}$ pipeline with zero additional latency, zero additional memory, and no access to scene geometry. {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\subsection{Discussion}\\n\\\\label{sec:discussion}\\n\\nThe design of \\\\ours{} draws a structural analogy to guidance distillation in image generation \\\\citep{meng2023distillation}, where classifier-free guidance is internalized into model weights during training so that inference proceeds without guidance computation. In our setting, the SDF auxiliary head plays the role of the guidance signal: it shapes the velocity field during training, then is discarded at inference. This ``train-with, infer-without'' paradigm is not specific to collision avoidance. The same framework could, in principle, internalize other auxiliary objectives (task progress metrics, energy efficiency constraints, human comfort zones) by substituting the SDF head with a corresponding prediction target and shaping loss. The key requirement is that the auxiliary signal be expressible as a per-timestep scalar or low-dimensional quantity computable from the model's internal representations.\\n\\nOur current implementation assumes access to analytical scene geometry for SDF label computation, which is readily available in simulation (MuJoCo mesh models) but not in unstructured real-world environments. Deformable objects, liquids, and transparent surfaces lack well-defined signed distance fields under the classical formulation. Learned SDF representations, such as DeepSDF \\\\citep{park2019deepsdf} or neural implicit fields estimated from depth sensors or point clouds, offer a plausible extension path, though the effect of label noise from such estimators on the collision-shaping loss remains an open question. A related limitation concerns the scope of the safety guarantee. The collision-shaping loss operates on per-timestep signed distance values and does not directly optimize trajectory-level safety constraints. Constrained RL methods such as SCPO \\\\citep{zhao2024scpo} and SafeVLA \\\\citep{zhang2025safevla} can optimize cumulative safety cost over a full episode and thus provide stronger multi-step collision avoidance guarantees. However, these methods lack explicit geometric grounding and may struggle with precise spatial reasoning near obstacle boundaries. A hybrid approach that combines \\\\ours{}'s per-timestep geometric shaping with trajectory-level constrained optimization could address both limitations.\\n\\nFrom a scalability perspective, the SDF auxiliary head adds only ${\\\\sim}$263K parameters to the 7B+ base model, a negligible overhead ($<$0.004\\\\%). The SDF labels are scalar quantities computed offline from simulator geometry; no dense 3D field storage or online computation is needed during training. These properties suggest that the approach scales naturally to larger VLA backbones (such as the full $\\\\pi_0$ at 3B+ parameters) and to environments with more complex obstacle configurations, since the per-timestep label cost remains constant regardless of model or environment scale.\\n\\n\\n%=============================================================================\\n\\\\section{Conclusion}\\n\\\\label{sec:conclusion}\\n%=============================================================================\\n\\nThis paper presented \\\\ours{}, a training-time method that embeds geometric collision awareness directly into VLA flow-matching weights through SDF auxiliary supervision and a noise-aware collision-shaping loss. Unlike prior approaches that impose safety at inference time through guided sampling \\\\citep{song2026omniguide}, post-hoc trajectory correction \\\\citep{hu2025aegis}, or constrained reinforcement learning \\\\citep{zhang2025safevla}, \\\\ours{} produces a model whose standard forward pass generates inherently collision-aware actions with zero additional latency, memory, or access to scene geometry at deployment.\\n\\nOur work makes three contributions. First, we showed that SDF collision geometry can be internalized into the velocity field of a flow-matching VLA during training, and that the SDF auxiliary head used for this purpose can be discarded entirely at inference. The resulting model runs through the unmodified $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ pipeline, yet its learned velocity field routes action trajectories away from collision boundaries. Second, we introduced a noise-aware collision-shaping loss whose time-dependent weight $w(t) = \\\\sigma(\\\\alpha(t_0 - t))$ activates the collision penalty only in the low-noise regime where the model's geometric predictions carry reliable spatial information. Third, we evaluated \\\\ours{} on SafeLIBERO (32 collision scenarios across 4 task suites) and four standard LIBERO suites, demonstrating \\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\% collision avoidance rate (compared to \\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\% for the vanilla $\\\\pi_{0.5}$) while maintaining \\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\% task success rate. This result validates a conceptual bridge between guidance distillation in image generation and safety internalization in VLA: the ``train-with, infer-without'' principle extends to geometric safety constraints for robot action generation.\\n\\nSeveral directions remain open. The current approach relies on SDF labels precomputed from simulator geometry; extending \\\\ours{} to real-world settings will require neural SDF estimation from depth observations or point clouds. Evaluating \\\\ours{} on additional VLA backbones beyond $\\\\pi_{0.5}$, such as Octo and RT-2, would establish the generality of the internalization principle. Finally, real-world deployment with truly novel object categories and deformable or transparent obstacles remains an important validation step.\\n\\n\\\\subsection*{Limitations}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Single benchmark.} All experiments were conducted on LIBERO and SafeLIBERO in the MuJoCo simulator. Validating \\\\ours{} on additional benchmarks such as ManiSkill, RoboCasa, or RLBench would strengthen confidence in its generality.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Single VLA backbone.} We evaluated \\\\ours{} exclusively on $\\\\pi_{0.5}$. Whether the collision-shaping signal transfers effectively to alternative architectures (diffusion-based policies like Octo, autoregressive action generation in RT-2) is an empirical question that this work does not address.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Known geometry assumption.} Ground-truth SDF labels were computed from MuJoCo's analytical geometry engine. In real-world deployment, scene geometry must be estimated from sensory input, and the impact of label noise on the collision-shaping loss has not been characterized.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Collision threshold sensitivity.} The safety margin $\\\\delta = 2$\\\\,cm was held fixed across all tasks. An adaptive or task-conditioned threshold could address scenarios with varying proximity requirements.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Simulation only.} All results are from simulated environments. A real-robot validation is needed to confirm that the internalized collision awareness survives the sim-to-real gap.\\n\\n\\\\subsection*{Broader Impact}\\n\\nSafe robot manipulation is a prerequisite for deploying autonomous robots in human-occupied environments. \\\\ours{} contributes by embedding collision awareness into VLA weights at training time, removing inference-time computational overhead. We note that the zero-overhead framing may create a false sense of completeness: training-time safety internalization complements rather than replaces runtime safety monitoring, particularly in high-stakes applications.\\n\\n\\n\\\\bibliography{references}\\n\\\\bibliographystyle{plainnat}\\n\\n\\\\appendix\\n\\\\input{appendix}\\n\\n\\\\end{document}\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.73}}"}

Unlike prior approaches that impose safety at inference time through guided sampling \\\\\\\\citep{song2026omniguide}, post-hoc trajectory correction \\\\\\\\citep{hu2025aegis}, or constrained reinforcement learning \\\\\\\\citep{zhang2025safevla}, \\\\\\\\ours{} produces a model whose standard forward pass generates inherently collision-aware actions with zero additional latency, memory, or access to scene geometry at deployment.\\\\n\\\\nOur work makes three contributions. First, we showed that SDF collision geometry can be internalized into the velocity field of a flow-matching VLA during training, and that the SDF auxiliary head used for this purpose can be discarded entirely at inference. The resulting model runs through the unmodified $\\\\\\\\pi_{0.5}$ pipeline, yet its learned velocity field routes action trajectories away from collision boundaries. Second, we introduced a noise-aware collision-shaping loss whose time-dependent weight $w(t) = \\\\\\\\sigma(\\\\\\\\alpha(t_0 - t))$ activates the collision penalty only in the low-noise regime where the model's geometric predictions carry reliable spatial information. Third, we evaluated \\\\\\\\ours{} on SafeLIBERO (32 collision scenarios across 4 task suites) and four standard LIBERO suites, demonstrating \\\\\\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\\\\\% collision avoidance rate (compared to \\\\\\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\\\\\% for the vanilla $\\\\\\\\pi_{0.5}$) while maintaining \\\\\\\\placeholder{XX}\\\\\\\\% task success rate. This result validates a conceptual bridge between guidance distillation in image generation and safety internalization in VLA: the ``train-with, infer-without'' principle extends to geometric safety constraints for robot action generation.\\\\n\\\\nSeveral directions remain open. The current approach relies on SDF labels precomputed from simulator geometry; extending \\\\\\\\ours{} to real-world settings will require neural SDF estimation from depth observations or point clouds. Evaluating \\\\\\\\ours{} on additional VLA backbones beyond $\\\\\\\\pi_{0.5}$, such as Octo and RT-2, would establish the generality of the internalization principle. Finally, real-world deployment with truly novel object categories and deformable or transparent obstacles remains an important validation step.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\subsection*{Limitations}\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\paragraph{Single benchmark.} All experiments were conducted on LIBERO and SafeLIBERO in the MuJoCo simulator. Validating \\\\\\\\ours{} on additional benchmarks such as ManiSkill, RoboCasa, or RLBench would strengthen confidence in its generality.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\paragraph{Single VLA backbone.} We evaluated \\\\\\\\ours{} exclusively on $\\\\\\\\pi_{0.5}$. Whether the collision-shaping signal transfers effectively to alternative architectures (diffusion-based policies like Octo, autoregressive action generation in RT-2) is an empirical question that this work does not address.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\paragraph{Known geometry assumption.} Ground-truth SDF labels were computed from MuJoCo's analytical geometry engine. In real-world deployment, scene geometry must be estimated from sensory input, and the impact of label noise on the collision-shaping loss has not been characterized.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\paragraph{Collision threshold sensitivity.} The safety margin $\\\\\\\\delta = 2$\\\\\\\\,cm was held fixed across all tasks. An adaptive or task-conditioned threshold could address scenarios with varying proximity requirements.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\paragraph{Simulation only.} All results are from simulated environments. A real-robot validation is needed to confirm that the internalized collision awareness survives the sim-to-real gap.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\subsection*{Broader Impact}\\\\n\\\\nSafe robot manipulation is a prerequisite for deploying autonomous robots in human-occupied environments. \\\\\\\\ours{} contributes by embedding collision awareness into VLA weights at training time, removing inference-time computational overhead. We note that the zero-overhead framing may create a false sense of completeness: training-time safety internalization complements rather than replaces runtime safety monitoring, particularly in high-stakes applications.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\bibliography{references}\\\\n\\\\\\\\bibliographystyle{plainnat}\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\appendix\\\\n\\\\\\\\input{appendix}\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\end{document}\\\\n\\\", \\\"stderr\\\": \\\"\\\", \\\"return_code\\\": 0}, \\\"error\\\": null, \\\"metadata\\\": {\\\"elapsed_seconds\\\": 0.73}}\"}",

      "result": "{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\subsection{Discussion}\\n\\\\label{sec:discussion}\\n\\nThe design of \\\\ours{} draws a structural analogy to guidance distillation in image generation \\\\citep{meng2023distillation}, where classifier-free guidance is internalized into model weights during training so that inference proceeds without guidance computation. In our setting, the SDF auxiliary head plays the role of the guidance signal: it shapes the velocity field during training, then is discarded at inference. This ``train-with, infer-without'' paradigm is not specific to collision avoidance. The same framework could, in principle, internalize other auxiliary objectives (task progress metrics, energy efficiency constraints, human comfort zones) by substituting the SDF head with a corresponding prediction target and shaping loss. The key requirement is that the auxiliary signal be expressible as a per-timestep scalar or low-dimensional quantity computable from the model's internal representations.\\n\\nOur current implementation assumes access to analytical scene geometry for SDF label computation, which is readily available in simulation (MuJoCo mesh models) but not in unstructured real-world environments. Deformable objects, liquids, and transparent surfaces lack well-defined signed distance fields under the classical formulation. Learned SDF representations, such as DeepSDF \\\\citep{park2019deepsdf} or neural implicit fields estimated from depth sensors or point clouds, offer a plausible extension path, though the effect of label noise from such estimators on the collision-shaping loss remains an open question. A related limitation concerns the scope of the safety guarantee. The collision-shaping loss operates on per-timestep signed distance values and does not directly optimize trajectory-level safety constraints. Constrained RL methods such as SCPO \\\\citep{zhao2024scpo} and SafeVLA \\\\citep{zhang2025safevla} can optimize cumulative safety cost over a full episode and thus provide stronger multi-step collision avoidance guarantees. However, these methods lack explicit geometric grounding and may struggle with precise spatial reasoning near obstacle boundaries. A hybrid approach that combines \\\\ours{}'s per-timestep geometric shaping with trajectory-level constrained optimization could address both limitations.\\n\\nFrom a scalability perspective, the SDF auxiliary head adds only ${\\\\sim}$263K parameters to the 7B+ base model, a negligible overhead ($<$0.004\\\\%). The SDF labels are scalar quantities computed offline from simulator geometry; no dense 3D field storage or online computation is needed during training. These properties suggest that the approach scales naturally to larger VLA backbones (such as the full $\\\\pi_0$ at 3B+ parameters) and to environments with more complex obstacle configurations, since the per-timestep label cost remains constant regardless of model or environment scale.\\n\\n\\n%=============================================================================\\n\\\\section{Conclusion}\\n\\\\label{sec:conclusion}\\n%=============================================================================\\n\\nThis paper presented \\\\ours{}, a training-time method that embeds geometric collision awareness directly into VLA flow-matching weights through SDF auxiliary supervision and a noise-aware collision-shaping loss. Unlike prior approaches that impose safety at inference time through guided sampling \\\\citep{song2026omniguide}, post-hoc trajectory correction \\\\citep{hu2025aegis}, or constrained reinforcement learning \\\\citep{zhang2025safevla}, \\\\ours{} produces a model whose standard forward pass generates inherently collision-aware actions with zero additional latency, memory, or access to scene geometry at deployment.\\n\\nOur work makes three contributions. First, we showed that SDF collision geometry can be internalized into the velocity field of a flow-matching VLA during training, and that the SDF auxiliary head used for this purpose can be discarded entirely at inference. Second, we introduced a noise-aware collision-shaping loss whose time-dependent weight $w(t) = \\\\sigma(\\\\alpha(t_0 - t))$ activates the collision penalty only in the low-noise regime where the model's geometric predictions carry reliable spatial information. This result validates a conceptual bridge between guidance distillation in image generation and safety internalization in VLA: the ``train-with, infer-without'' principle extends to geometric safety constraints for robot action generation.\\n\\nSeveral directions remain open. The current approach relies on SDF labels precomputed from simulator geometry; extending \\\\ours{} to real-world settings will require neural SDF estimation from depth observations or point clouds. Evaluating \\\\ours{} on additional VLA backbones beyond $\\\\pi_{0.5}$, such as Octo and RT-2, would establish the generality of the internalization principle. {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"199:\\\\paragraph{Base model and training.} We build on $\\\\pi_{0.5}$, the open-source flow-matching VLA released by Physical Intelligence via the openpi codebase. All experiments use full fine-tuning of the entire model (not LoRA adapters), as the available GPU memory permits this and full fine-tuning provides a stronger baseline for isolating the effect of collision shaping. Training runs for 30,000 gradient steps on two NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96\\\\,GB VRAM each) using PyTorch Distributed Data Parallel (DDP). The per-GPU batch size is 64, yielding an effective batch size of 128. We use AdamW with a linear warmup of 10,000 steps to a peak learning rate of $5 \\\\times 10^{-5}$, followed by cosine decay. Gradients from all parameters (base model and SDF head jointly) are clipped by global norm with $\\\\mathrm{max\\\\_norm} = 1.0$. The vanilla $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ model is trained with the identical configuration but without the SDF auxiliary head or collision-shaping loss.\\n271:\\\\paragraph{SDF prediction accuracy.} We evaluate the SDF head's prediction quality across training by computing the mean absolute error between predicted and ground-truth signed distances on a held-out validation set. Prediction error is \\\\placeholder{X.X}\\\\,cm at step 500 and \\\\placeholder{X.X}\\\\,cm at step 30,000 for the full model.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.45}}"}

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"199:\\\\paragraph{Base model and training.} We build on $\\\\pi_{0.5}$, the open-source flow-matching VLA released by Physical Intelligence via the openpi codebase. All experiments use full fine-tuning of the entire model (not LoRA adapters), as the available GPU memory permits this and full fine-tuning provides a stronger baseline for isolating the effect of collision shaping. Training runs for 30,000 gradient steps on two NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96\\\\,GB VRAM each) using PyTorch Distributed Data Parallel (DDP). The per-GPU batch size is 64, yielding an effective batch size of 128. We use AdamW with a linear warmup of 10,000 steps to a peak learning rate of $5 \\\\times 10^{-5}$, followed by cosine decay. Gradients from all parameters (base model and SDF head jointly) are clipped by global norm with $\\\\mathrm{max\\\\_norm} = 1.0$. The vanilla $\\\\pi_{0.5}$ model is trained with the identical configuration but without the SDF auxiliary head or collision-shaping loss.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.45}}"}
